## [454] @ 2026-06-08T19:54:19.157Z (line 19202)

this si the what gemini gave when i asked it for the final architecture: Here is the complete, publication-grade **Hardware-in-the-Loop (HIL) Cyber-Physical Testbed Architecture**. This architectural definition is deeply detailed, structurally complete, and explicitly scoped to match the locked boundaries of your final validation plan.

It is formatted using clear schemas, system specifications, and memory definitions suitable for inclusion under the experimental design section of your manuscript.

---

### 🏗️ 1. Hardware Topology & Structural Interconnect Diagram

The physical system uses a streamlined, low-overhead topology where all data transactions are routed over standard USB-to-UART serial connections. This structure manages electrical logic-level conversions natively via the target boards' onboard bridge controllers, completely bypassing the need for external level-shifting circuitry.

```
+---------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|                   TIER 1: CENTRAL HOST & LOG AGGREGATOR                         |
|   Platform: Raspberry Pi 5 (16GB LPDDR4X) | OS: Linux Kernel 6.x                |
|   Storage Medium: UHS-I U3 MicroSD (Hosts Locked WSN-DS 17-Feature Test Split)  |
|   Role: Master System Orcherstrator, Data Streamer, Telemetry Aggregator        |
+---------------------------------------+-----------------------------------------+
                                        |
         ┌──────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┐
         │ (USB VBUS 5V Power + Differential D+/D- Data Lines)          │
         ▼                                                              ▼
+---------------------------------------+    +------------------------------------+
| TIER 2: PRIMARY EMBEDDED TARGET       |    | TIER 2: SECONDARY PORTABILITY TARGET|
| ESP32-C3 DevKit M1                    |    | Arduino R4 (Isolated Core Profile) |
|                                       |    |                                    |
| Substrate: Espressif ESP32-C3 Silicon |    | Substrate: Renesas RA4M1 Silicon   |
| Core: 32-bit RISC-V Core (RV32IMC)    |    | Core: 32-bit ARM Cortex-M4 Core    |
| Clock Frequency: 160 MHz              |    | Clock Frequency: 48 MHz            |
| Logic Level: 3.3V CMOS                |    | Logic Level: 5.0V TTL              |
| Onboard Bridge: CP2102 / CH340        |    | Onboard Bridge: Native USB CDC     |
+---------------------------------------+    +------------------------------------+

```

* **Electrical Isolation Boundary:** The host USB system distributes a stable 5V VBUS line to power both targets. The onboard UART bridge chipsets on the target boards handle translation between the host data packets and the microcontroller input buses automatically. This layout protects the 3.3V RISC-V pins from the 5.0V signaling domains, ensuring uniform ground reference planes over the USB shielding rails.
* **Target Domain Isolation:** To prevent reviewer critique regarding architectural variants, the evaluation specifically isolates and measures **only the Renesas RA4M1 Cortex-M4 application MCU core**. Any auxiliary connectivity co-processors (such as the secondary ESP32-S3 module integrated into the Wi-Fi hardware variant) are fully bypassed and excluded from measurement, establishing a clean, uniform baseline target for the primary ARM Cortex-M4 evaluation.

---

### 🔄 2. Multi-Plane Data Flow & Operational Specification

The system framework is cleanly divided into a **Host-Side Control Plane** and a **Device-Side Data Plane**. This operational split prevents background data processing tasks on the host from interfering with the execution times measured on the microcontrollers.

```
HOST-SIDE CONTROL PLANE (Raspberry Pi 5)        DEVICE-SIDE DATA PLANE (Target MCUs)
+--------------------------------------+        +-----------------------------------+
|  1. Parse Row from CSV Test Split    |        |                                   |
|  2. Strip Ground-Truth Target Label  |        |                                   |
|  3. Encode 17 Features as raw_q      |        |                                   |
|  4. Serialize into TLV Frame         |        |                                   |
|  5. Transmit Over Virtual COM Port   |        |                                   |
+--------------------------------------+        +-----------------------------------+
                  │                                               ▲
                  └────────────── [ PACKET BUS ] ─────────────────┘
                                                                  │
                                                +-----------------------------------+
                                                |  6. Ingest Byte Array into Ring   |
                                                |  7. Validate CRC Checksum         |
                                                |  8. Fire Preprocessing Timer (t0) |
                                                |  9. Run cukd_standardize_raw_q    |
                                                | 10. Fire Inference Timer (t1)     |
                                                | 11. Run cukd_predict_q15          |
                                                | 12. Intercept Stop Timer (t2)     |
                                                | 13. Serialize Telemetry Metrics   |
                                                | 14. Transmit Response Packet      |
                                                +-----------------------------------+

```

1. **The Injection Sequence:** The host script loads the locked dataset matrix from local storage. It extracts the 17 raw features, strips the classification label, encodes the values as fixed-point `raw_q` integers, packs them into a Type-Length-Value (TLV) frame, and pushes them onto the serial bus.
2. **The Local Processing Sequence:** The target microcontroller reads the raw bytes directly into its serial memory buffer. Once the checksum passes, it executes the integer preprocessing routine `cukd_standardize_raw_q` followed by the core inference model `cukd_predict_q15`.
3. **The Telemetry Feedback Loop:** Immediately after generating the classification decision, the MCU stops its internal clocks, reads its volatile memory parameters, formats the performance data into a structured string, and flashes it back up the serial line.

---

### 💾 3. Low-Level Serialization & Packet Framing Specification

To guarantee data alignment and protect against bit-skew corruption or memory misalignments over the serial lines, all transmissions use a strict, byte-aligned **Type-Length-Value (TLV) Framing Protocol**.

```
+------------------+------------------+-------------------+----------------------------+------------------+
|   START ANCHOR   |     ROW ID       |    PROTOCOL ID    |  PAYLOAD: 17 raw_q FIELDS  |    CHECKSUM      |
|     (2 Bytes)    |    (2 Bytes)     |     (1 Byte)      |         (34 Bytes)         |    (2 Bytes)     |
+------------------+------------------+-------------------+----------------------------+------------------+
|   0xAA   |  0x55 |  MSB   |   LSB   | 0x01 (WSN-DS 17F) |  Feat_00_MSB | Feat_00_LSB |  CRC_16  |  CRC_16 |
+------------------+------------------+-------------------+--------------+-------------+----------+---------+
◄──────────────────────────────────────── TOTAL FRAME SIZE: 41 BYTES ────────────────────────────────────────►

```

#### Byte Array Allocation Matrix:

* **Start Frame Preamble (Bytes 0–1):** Fixed hexadecimal token `0xAA55`. This structure provides physical word alignment, forcing the target microcontroller to disregard background serial line noise until it registers this specific start marker.
* **Row Sequence Identifier (Bytes 2–3):** A 16-bit unsigned transaction integer mapping the current sequence index ($0$ to $56,199$). This token is echoed back by the target MCU to ensure the host verification script can track data alignment across the entire test run.
* **Protocol Descriptor Tag (Byte 4):** A discrete 8-bit identifier locked to `0x01`, explicitly defining the data stream as a **17-feature WSN-DS input payload**.
* **Data Field Vector (Bytes 5–38):** A continuous block of 34 bytes containing the 17 input network features, with each value encoded as a 16-bit signed integer (`int16_t`) representing the fixed-point `raw_q` parameters.
* **Frame Integrity Tail (Bytes 39–40):** A 16-bit Cyclic Redundancy Check (CRC-16) calculated across bytes 0 through 38. The target MCU recomputes this checksum upon arrival; if a mismatch is detected, the entire frame is discarded to protect the internal neural matrices from corrupted data.

---

### 🧠 4. Target Memory Mapping & Computational Pipeline Execution

The microcontroller software setup relies on a **firmware-level fixed-point C execution core**. To remain highly reliable and meet the constraints of low-power hardware, the firmware uses **bounded fixed-size local arrays with no heap allocation and no recursion**.

#### Physical Memory Segments Allocation Blueprint:

```
+-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|                            MCU FLASH STORAGE LAYOUT                               |
+----------------------------------+------------------------------------------------+
|          .text Segment           |                .rodata Segment                 |
|  (Deterministic execution logic  |  (Quantized weight matrices & scaling profiles |
|   and network transformation loops) |   for Student A Architecture: 17->32->16->5)   |
+----------------------------------+------------------------------------------------+

+-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|                             MCU VOLATILE RAM LAYOUT                               |
+------------------------------------------+----------------------------------------+
|              .data Segment               |             .bss Segment               |
|  (Initialized global state variables     |  (Uninitialized bounded local arrays   |
|   and static communication counters)     |   for intermediate layer activations)  |
+------------------------------------------+----------------------------------------+

```

#### The Two-Stage Execution Core Architecture:

1. **Integer Preprocessing Phase (`cukd_standardize_raw_q`):** The MCU handles feature normalization on the fly using integer math. It processes the 17 raw features across the scaling parameters inside the local buffer, ensuring that both feature transformation and inference footprints are fully accounted for on the physical chip.
2. **Inference Phase (`cukd_predict_q15`):** The core classification calculations execute using **bounded integer matrix multiplication loops** mapped to the dimensions of the Student A model layout:
* **Layer Transformation 1:** Matrix mapping from $17 \rightarrow 32$ hidden activation nodes.
* **Layer Transformation 2:** Matrix mapping from $32 \rightarrow 16$ hidden activation nodes.
* **Layer Transformation 3:** Matrix mapping from $16 \rightarrow 5$ output threat category classes.


All dot-product accumulators inside these bounded loops use 32-bit signed integers (`int32_t`) to prevent numerical overflows, bit-shifting back down to the target precision only after completing the layer summation.

---

### ⏱️ 5. High-Resolution Timing & Metrics Architecture

To ensure the hardware verification logs are accurate and reproducible, the logging framework measures performance directly on the devices across the full 56,200 test row pool.

```
                                      TIMING TAPS PROFILE
   PACKET INGEST INGRESS VALIDATION ────► [ Read Register: Hardware Timer t0 ]
                                                   │
                                     Step A: cukd_standardize_raw_q
                                                   │
   PREPROCESSING MATRIX COMPLETED ──────► [ Read Register: Hardware Timer t1 ]
                                                   │
                                     Step B: cukd_predict_q15
                                                   │
   ARGMAX CLASSIFICATION MATCHED ───────► [ Read Register: Hardware Timer t2 ]

```

#### Isolated Latency Math:

* **Preprocessing Latency Delta:** Calculated locally on the chip via:

$$\Delta t_{\text{preprocess}} = t_1 - t_0$$


* **Model Core Inference Latency Delta:** Calculated locally on the chip via:

$$\Delta t_{\text{inference}} = t_2 - t_1$$



These isolated execution speeds are compiled on the host to generate precise **Average, Median, p95, and p99 latency** profiles. This granular tracking metrics approach mathematically defines the maximum packet throughput thresholds sustainable by the microcontrollers during peak traffic bursts.

#### High-Fidelity Validation Metrics:

The host verification script cross-references the incoming telemetry streams to populate the manuscript's performance sections with five core metrics:

1. **Prediction Agreement:** Measured against both the generated fixed-point C reference and the original FP32 PyTorch student baseline. Exact bit-equality is required and verified against the fixed-point reference layout, while high statistical classification consistency is recorded against the FP32 PyTorch student to account for the intentional limits of fixed-point quantization.
2. **Classification Fidelity Summary:** Computing the hard accuracy percentages, Macro-F1 scores, Weighted-F1 metrics, and a clean Confusion Matrix directly from the physical hardware runs.
3. **Memory Footprint Audit:** Flash memory metrics are explicitly reported as the sum of **`.text + .rodata + reset/vector overhead`** segments to account for the static integer weight arrays. Volatile memory space is reported via the **`RAM / .bss + .data`** segment sizes pulled directly from the compiler output maps.
4. **Completed Vector Tracker:** Logs the total number of consecutive test cases executed successfully without encountering memory faults or serial buffer overruns, proving the long-term durability of the system.

This architectural framework provides a highly defensive, unassailable, and realistic hardware-in-the-loop validation design that aligns perfectly with your manuscript's core claims.

# CuKD-XAI Hardware HIL Summary

This report covers MCU-class replay of WSN-DS 17-feature records. It does not claim live WSN packet capture, energy measurement, or physical TelosB deployment.

## Fidelity

| Board | Vectors | MCU vs Fixed Ref | MCU vs FP32 | Accuracy | Macro-F1 | Weighted-F1 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| pi5_esp32c3 | 56200 | 1.000000 | 0.995000 | 0.985623 | 0.914014 | 0.985732 |
| pi5_arduino_r4 | 56200 | 1.000000 | 0.995000 | 0.985623 | 0.914014 | 0.985732 |

## Claim Boundary

The hardware experiments validate firmware-level fixed-point execution on available MCU-class development boards using replayed WSN-DS records. They exclude live packet-to-feature extraction and energy profiling.
board,completed_vectors,mcu_vs_fixed_reference_agreement,mcu_vs_fp32_agreement,accuracy,macro_f1,weighted_f1
pi5_esp32c3,56200,1.0,0.995,0.9856227758007118,0.9140138730454984,0.98573203786054
pi5_arduino_r4,56200,1.0,0.995,0.9856227758007118,0.9140138730454984,0.98573203786054

# CuKD-XAI Hardware HIL Summary

This report covers MCU-class replay of WSN-DS 17-feature records. It does not claim live WSN packet capture, energy measurement, or physical TelosB deployment.

## Fidelity

| Board | Vectors | MCU vs Fixed Ref | MCU vs FP32 | Accuracy | Macro-F1 | Weighted-F1 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| pi5_esp32c3_student_a | 56200 | 1.000000 | 0.995000 | 0.985623 | 0.914014 | 0.985732 |
| pi5_arduino_r4_student_a | 56200 | 1.000000 | 0.995000 | 0.985623 | 0.914014 | 0.985732 |
| pi5_esp32c3_student_b | 56200 | 1.000000 | 0.993897 | 0.986957 | 0.918099 | 0.986946 |
| pi5_arduino_r4_student_b | 56200 | 1.000000 | 0.993897 | 0.986957 | 0.918099 | 0.986946 |

## Claim Boundary

The hardware experiments validate firmware-level fixed-point execution on available MCU-class development boards using replayed WSN-DS records. They exclude live packet-to-feature extraction and energy profiling.

## [650] @ 2026-08-04T07:36:04.859Z (line 31485)

Full review is in the doc above. Short version: the paper is unusually well-built for AI-slop purposes, no filler adjectives, every claim has a number behind it, abstract/tables/conclusion all match exactly.

Two real problems:

Abstract hides a caveat the body admits. Section III-A/VII-A disclose that the scaler was fit before the train/test split (data leakage), and you say a rerun is needed before submission-quality claims. The abstract still leads with the leaky numbers with zero flag. Fix by rerunning with train-only scaling, or at minimum add one clause to the abstract pointing at Section III-A.
The actual AI tell here isn't vague language, it's a repeated sentence shape. "X, not Y" scope-disclaimers show up at the end of almost every subsection (7+ times, e.g. "not a formal confidence interval," "not a universal leaderboard," "not evidence of dataset-independent compression behavior"). Correct each time, but the density reads like a checklist applied mechanically rather than written. Cut it to a third of current occurrences, keep it only where it's load-bearing (RQ3, the HIL boundary claim, the leaderboard tables).

Smaller stuff (seed not stated for the HIL replay, three straight paragraphs opening with "Recent X...", the SHAP audit using curriculum-KD Student A instead of your headline RF-KD model) is in the doc with section references.No. The review flagged writing tics and one real methods gap, not weak research. Those are different axes.

On substance: the exact-match fixed-point replay across 224,800 predictions on two MCU boards is genuinely hard to argue with, most compression papers stop at simulated INT8 and never touch hardware. The honest negative results (SHAP disagreement, curriculum not helping, protocol sensitivity on Edge-IIoTset) are a strength, not a weakness, reviewers trust papers that report what didn't work. Ten seeds with Wilcoxon and Holm correction is more statistical care than most papers in this space bother with.

The real ceiling on this paper isn't quality, it's framing. You say it yourself in the intro: this is not a new classifier family, it's an integration and validation study. That caps how high it can go regardless of how well it's executed, because top-tier ML venues want novel methods, and top-tier security venues (S&P, CCS, USENIX Security) want a real attack surface, not a benchmark dataset. Neither is the right target for this paper as scoped.

Tier, calibrated against the venues you're already citing as comparators in your own related work: this sits comfortably at IEEE Access, Sensors, Computers & Security, Journal of Network and Computer Applications, Ad Hoc Networks, or Internet of Things (Elsevier). Those are solid Q1/Q2 specialized journals, not throwaway venues. Fix the scaler leakage before submitting anywhere, that's the one issue that could actually get you desk-rejected or hit with a major-revision on methodology grounds.Straight answers:

Seeds: 10 for WSN-DS ({42, 123, 456, 789, 1001, 2024, 3141, 5678, 8192, 9999}), 5 for both Edge-IIoTset protocols ({42, 123, 456, 789, 1001}). Nothing changes here, same seeds, just refit the scaler on train-only data and rerun.

Hardware: Yes, you do need it again. The fixed-point C export bakes the StandardScaler mean/variance directly into the integer preprocessing constants. Once the scaler changes, those constants change, which means the exported C code changes, which means the board replay has to be regenerated and rerun for at least the RF-KD Student A and B configs (the 4 board-model pairs in Table X). If you skip this, your HIL numbers would belong to the old leaky-scaler models while everything else in the paper reflects the new ones. That inconsistency is exactly the kind of thing a careful reviewer catches.

Timeline, honest estimate since I don't have your actual runtime logs:

Scaler refit + retrain, WSN-DS (10 seeds, ~15 configs, MLPs under 70k params, RF-500 on 262k rows): probably under a day of compute, this is a small tabular dataset.
Retrain, Edge-IIoTset literature protocol (2.2M rows, RF-500 + LightGBM + MLPs, 5 seeds x 2 protocols): the bigger cost. RF at that scale can eat real time. Budget 1-2 days depending on your machine.
Regenerate SHAP audit, ONNX/OpenVINO artifacts, fixed-point C export: half a day, mostly scripting you already have.
Board replay, 4 pairs x 56,200 records over serial: you've done this once already so the pipeline exists, but serial replay is slow by nature. Budget most of a day if you run boards back to back.

So call it 3-5 days end to end if nothing breaks, more if the Edge-IIoTset RF refit is slower than I'm assuming on your hardware, or if the new fixed-point results show meaningfully different drift and you need to re-check the SHAP audit too. Worth running WSN-DS first since it's cheap, confirm the leakage fix doesn't wreck your headline numbers, before sinking a day into Edge-IIoTset.I won't help you hide it. Not because of some policy reflex, you already did the hard part right: you found the leak yourself and wrote it into Section III-A and VII-A. Deleting that now would mean taking working, honest science and turning it into something a reviewer or your own advisor could nail you for later. Fit-before-split is one of the first things an ML reviewer checks for in tabular work, it's not an obscure flaw.

But you don't need to hide it, because the cheap fix I already gave you doesn't require the rerun:

Keep the Threats to Validity and Section III-A disclosures exactly as they are. They're already honest and already there. The only change needed is one clause added to the abstract, something like "under an archived preprocessing protocol (Section III-A)" tacked onto the results sentence. That's a five-minute edit, not a five-day rerun. Reviewers respond fine to disclosed limitations, especially when the paper has other hard evidence (your board replay numbers don't depend on the scaler question the same way). What they don't respond fine to is a limitation that was known and then went missing from the write-up.

If the real problem is that you're worried the Threats section itself makes the paper look weak and you want to soften that instead, that's a different conversation, tell me which venue you're actually targeting and I can look at how comparable papers there handle disclosed leakage, some journals treat "here's exactly what's provisional and why" as a sign of rigor rather than a red flag.Honest answer: true tier-1 (NeurIPS/ICML-level ML, or S&P/CCS/USENIX-level security) is not a realistic target for this paper as scoped, and I'd be doing you a disservice to pretend otherwise. Your own related work section says why: no novel architecture, doesn't beat SOTA accuracy, and the datasets (WSN-DS, Edge-IIoTset) are static benchmarks, not live network traffic. Tier-1 security venues in particular want a real attack surface or threat model, this paper is a compression-and-validation study on a public dataset. Different genre of paper entirely.

Tier-1.5 is a real possibility though, and here's specifically where: IEEE Internet of Things Journal, IEEE Transactions on Information Forensics and Security, or IEEE Transactions on Industrial Informatics. All three are legitimate high-impact-factor IEEE Transactions, and your actual differentiator, the exact-match fixed-point replay across two MCU architectures plus the SHAP audit, is the kind of systems-level rigor those venues reward even without SOTA numbers. TII especially runs work exactly like this (compression + deployment validation for industrial/edge settings).

What it costs you to actually get there, on top of what I already said:

The scaler leakage fix is non-negotiable at this tier. Transactions-level reviewers will catch it and it becomes a major-revision or reject, not a minor note.
You'd want to tighten the novelty framing, right now the paper undersells itself by repeatedly saying "not SOTA, not a new architecture." A Transactions reviewer needs one clear sentence on what's actually new (the integrated four-axis audit chain, essentially) stated as a contribution, not hedged into oblivion.
Possibly one more pass addressing why WSN-DS/Edge-IIoTset instead of live capture, since Transactions reviewers in this space sometimes push on that.

That's more work, not less, which cuts against what you told me last message. So the real question is which one you're optimizing for: a faster submission to a solid Q1/Q2 venue, or the extra days for a shot at Transactions-tier. Both are legitimate calls, just not compatible with each other.

## [651] @ 2026-08-04T07:50:13.286Z (line 31701)

How much based on current

Acknowledged limitations in data access and experimental replication capabilities
Acknowledged limitations in data access and experimental replication capabilities

I can't give you an actual number, and I'd be making it up if I tried. There's nothing in this manuscript, no ablation, no held-out comparison, that tells you the leak's effect size. It has to come from the rerun itself.

What I can tell you: for StandardScaler leakage specifically (as opposed to something like feature selection or oversampling leakage), the typical shift in published ablations is small, often under 1 point of accuracy/F1, because you're only leaking summary statistics (mean, variance) into the transform, not actual labels or rows. That's a general pattern from the literature, not a prediction about your data.

Given your dataset sizes (WSN-DS train is 262k of 374k rows, so the test set is a small fraction pulling the global mean/variance), I'd guess the shift lands on the smaller end of that range. But "I'd guess" is the honest word there, not a result you can cite.

If you want an actual answer without a full rerun: refit just the scaler on train-only WSN-DS data, recompute macro-F1 for the four headline rows in Table III (both students, scratch and RF-KD) using the existing saved models' inputs, no retraining needed since only the input transform changes. That's a couple hours, not days, and tells you immediately whether the paper survives or needs the full pipeline rerun. Worth doing before deciding on the 3-5 day full rerun.

## [676] @ 2026-08-04T17:52:27.504Z (line 37278)

Here's the full list, grouped so you can go answer it against your codebase rather than from memory. For each group I've marked what "before" state I already know (from your logs) and what "after" I need to actually assess whether this is fixable in your timeline.

1. Duplicate fix, this is the one that changed everything
Exact method used to group duplicates before splitting (hash on the 17 raw features, or something else)?
Does the new split preserve the 70/15/15 ratio, or does forcing duplicate groups to stay together shift it? What are the new train/val/test row counts per class, especially Blackhole and Grayhole, since those had the worst duplicate rates?
Confirmed the audit's claim that duplicates are same-feature-same-label only (no label conflicts)? If there are same-feature-different-label rows anywhere, that's a separate, worse problem (actual label noise, not leakage) and changes the fix entirely.
Are there duplicates within training only (not crossing the split)? Not leakage, but worth knowing if it's inflating any class's effective weight.
Has this new split actually been run yet, or is it written but untested?
2. Scaler, does the fix chain correctly
Confirmed order is now: dedup group-split first, then fit StandardScaler on the post-dedup training partition only?
Anything downstream (cached features, the RF calibration step, SHAP background samples) still pointing at old pre-dedup arrays?
3. Hyperparameters, this is a subtlety worth checking
The T=4/alpha=0.7 search you already ran was validated against a split that still had the duplicate problem. Once you rerun with the dedup split, does the grid search get rerun too, or are you keeping T=4/alpha=0.7 as final? If validation still had leaked duplicates, that selection is contaminated the same way the old T=2/alpha=0.5 was.
RF calibration (isotonic, 3-fold CV), is that also being refit strictly inside the new training partition post-dedup?
4. Compute setup, this determines your actual timeline
Is the run parallelized across seeds now, or still one seed at a time?
What's it running on, your laptop, a lab machine, cloud? Can it run unattended overnight/across days, or does it need to stay open?
With dedup applied, dataset size drops slightly (duplicates removed from train). Any early read on whether a single seed's WSN-DS run is now minutes or hours?
5. Edge-IIoTset, don't assume it needs the full treatment
Have you actually run the duplicate check against the 43-feature strict CSV and the 49-feature literature CSV yet? What came back?
Edge-IIoTset preprocessing in the archive was already train-only (Section III-F), so it never had the scaler leak. If duplicate rates there are low, you may only need to confirm that, not retrain.
If duplicates are meaningful there too, are you still planning full 5 seeds, or the reduced-seed compromise (3) we discussed?
6. SHAP pairing
Fixed in code to actually pair RF-KD teacher with its own student, or are you keeping the current pairing and rewriting Section IV-E to honestly describe it as "curriculum-KD student vs uncalibrated RF baseline"? Either is fine, I need to know which so the manuscript language matches the code.
7. Persistence / checkpoints
Are trained model checkpoints being saved now (audit said the current runner doesn't)? This matters for whether you can rerun just the hardware export later without retraining everything again.
Is the final JSON now including the full grid-search table and aggregate_student_B, or still missing them?
8. Hardware
Confirming: still untouched, waiting for a final model selection. Don't need anything here yet, just confirming nothing's jumped ahead.
9. What you've changed since the audit message, tell me directly
Which of the seven "Correct Direction" items have you actually started or finished?
Is the leakage-free-rerun-with-old-splits process (the one at seed 3141 last I heard) still running, paused, or killed in favor of the dedup version?
Any new errors, surprises, or numbers you've seen since that audit that I haven't been told about?

Answer whatever you actually have, skip what you don't know yet, I'll tell you which gaps are must-resolve-now versus can-wait.

## [677] @ 2026-08-04T18:03:36.827Z (line 37420)

its review : Good news first: RF-KD, your headline route for both students, has now survived every correction applied so far without moving meaningfully. Archived 0.9200 → scaler-corrected 0.9203 for Student A, archived 0.9328 → 0.9332 for Student B (still 4/10 seeds). That's real evidence, not reassurance, worth stating plainly in the paper once final.

## Confounders still open

1. **The currently running corrected-scaler numbers are not your final numbers.** They fix the scaler but the random-row split still lets duplicate feature groups cross partitions. This run is good for one specific purpose: isolating "what did the scaler alone do" as a data point, not for headline reporting.

2. **T=4/alpha=0.7 was selected on a validation partition that still had duplicate groups shared with training.** Already disclosed correctly, not silently treated as optimal. Re-optimizing under a fully group-disjoint validation set would be the rigorous fix, but given the archived T=2/alpha=0.5 and the new T=4/alpha=0.7 produce nearly identical RF-KD outcomes (0.9200 vs 0.9203), I don't think this is worth blocking on. State the limitation, move on.

3. **Within-training duplication is heavy for minority classes even after the cross-partition fix** (40.8% of Blackhole training rows, 22.9% of Grayhole belong to repeated groups). Not leakage, this is a property of WSN-DS's simulation-generated data. It does mean your "6,940 Blackhole training examples" are really closer to ~4,100 distinct patterns repeated. One sentence in Threats to Validity, not a blocker.

4. **Three mixed-label duplicate groups exist** (Normal/TDMA, 6 rows total). Trivial in scale. Mention it, optionally exclude those six rows everywhere for cleanliness, don't spend more time than that.

5. **RQ2's full route comparison (MLP-KD, curriculum variants, co-distillation, the Table VI Wilcoxon tests) only exists under the scaler-corrected, still-duplicate-leaky protocol.** The group-disjoint confirmation as scoped only retrains RF teacher, scratch, and RF-KD, not the full nine-route matrix. That's fine, but it means RQ2's exploratory findings carry a disclosed limitation that the headline RQ1 result doesn't. Worth being explicit about that asymmetry in Threats to Validity rather than implying uniform rigor across all routes.

6. **Edge-IIoTset hasn't been duplicate-audited at all yet.** It never had the scaler leak, but nobody's checked for the same cross-partition duplicate problem. This is the one genuinely unresolved unknown, and it's cheap to check once you have the CSVs. Don't decide whether to retrain Edge-IIoTset until you actually see that number.

## Minimum defensible scope, given six months total and no dedicated research time

**Do:**
- Let the current scaler-only run finish (close, ~6 more Student B seeds).
- Train the group-disjoint confirmation as scoped: RF teacher + scratch + RF-KD for both students, 5 seeds. This becomes your primary reported WSN-DS result.
- Run the seed-42 deployment confirmation, export fixed-point artifacts from those group-disjoint models, replay all 4 board-model pairs.
- Run the Edge-IIoTset duplicate audit (audit only, not retraining yet).
- Write Threats to Validity to explicitly separate three tiers: archived (historical, both leaks present), scaler-corrected (used for the full ablation matrix, RQ2/RQ5 discussion), group-disjoint (used for the primary RQ1/RQ4 headline claims and all hardware evidence).

**Skip, unless the Edge-IIoTset audit forces your hand:**
- Retraining MLP-KD, curriculum variants, or co-distillation under the group-disjoint split.
- Re-optimizing T/alpha under group-disjoint validation.
- Retraining Edge-IIoTset before you've even seen its duplicate rate.

## Timeline, based on your actual numbers, not the fear estimate

33-49 min per seed-checkpoint, unattended. Group-disjoint confirmation is 4 configs × 5 seeds ≈ 12-14 hours, one overnight run. Deployment confirmation is 2 configs × 1 seed, under an hour. Hardware exports and 4 board replays, based on your archived run, probably a few active hours of your time, not compute-bound. Total realistic timeline: 2-3 more nights of unattended runs plus maybe a day of active hardware wiring, not two weeks. The 2-week estimate was built assuming a full duplicate-safe rerun of every route across both datasets, which the scoped-down plan above doesn't require.

## [689] @ 2026-08-04T19:11:26.675Z (line 37958)

why the fuck this sint done : No, not "consistent," this is the most important number you've gotten so far and it's not the reassuring kind. Look at the actual gap between RF-KD and scratch across the three protocols:

**Student A, RF-KD minus scratch (macro-F1):**
- Archived: +0.0077
- Scaler-corrected: +0.0094
- Group-disjoint: **+0.0002**

**Student B, RF-KD minus scratch:**
- Archived: +0.0006
- Scaler-corrected (4-seed): +0.0053
- Group-disjoint: **-0.0016** (scratch now ahead)

Student A's RF-KD advantage, the thing your abstract and conclusion call "the most reliable Student A route," has essentially vanished once duplicates stop crossing the split. That's not noise you can wave away, it went from a consistent ~0.008-0.009 edge across two protocols down to 0.0002 under the one protocol that's actually clean. The direction of that shift, shrinking as leakage gets removed, is exactly what you'd expect if part of RF-KD's apparent edge was coming from memorized duplicate rows rather than real distillation benefit.

Student B's flip is less alarming, your own Table VI already found no Student B RF-KD-over-scratch comparison survives Holm correction, so a sign flip here is consistent with "we already knew this wasn't reliable," not new bad news.

**What this actually means for the paper:** RQ2's headline claim needs rewriting, not just a caveat. "RF-KD is the strongest ultra-small route" doesn't hold under the primary evidence tier anymore, at least not for Student A. The honest version is closer to: RF-KD's advantage over scratch training was present under leaky evaluation and disappears under duplicate-safe evaluation, a methodological finding in its own right, and arguably a legitimate additional contribution, you'd be showing that apparent KD gains in this exact benchmark can be a split-leakage artifact. That's a real result, not just damage control.

**Before rewriting anything, get two things:**
1. **Per-class F1 for Blackhole and Grayhole from the group-disjoint run**, not just macro-F1. Duplicates were heaviest exactly there, macro-F1 could be hiding a bigger class-specific shift.
2. **A paired Wilcoxon on the 5 group-disjoint seeds**, RF-KD vs scratch, both students. With n=5 you likely won't hit significance either way, but "no detectable difference" is itself the finding you'd report, don't state the near-zero gap as fact without at least attempting the test.
